```text
Good. Let me also check the existing training time estimates to calibrate GPU-hours.

From registry data:
- plan018-additive-random-b01-s44-r2: train_runtime_sec = 4998 (~83 min for 500 steps)
- plan018-additive-random-b01-s43-r3: not listed runtime but s42 had similar
- exp1-random-b03-s43-r4: train_runtime_sec = 6161.1 (~103 min)
- plan002-additive-b01-s43-r2: has eval data but no runtime listed


- plan001-exec-only-s43-1000-r2 and plan016-extended-1000-s42 both ran 1000 steps without recorded runtimes

So I can extrapolate: Qwen3-4B takes roughly 80-100 minutes per 500 steps on RTX PRO 6000, which scales to 160-200 minutes for 1000 steps. The smaller 1.5B variant should be about 40-60% faster at 50-60 minutes for 500 steps, while the 7B model would take 1.5-2× longer at 150-200 minutes for 500 steps.

Looking at my resource constraints now: I have two idle RTX PRO 6000 GPUs with 96GB each, 84GB free disk space, and 9 days until the May 25 deadline. Since I can run two experiments in parallel, I need to map out a realistic schedule. For week 2, I'm planning a Qwen3-1.5B pilot with 2 conditions and 3 seeds—that's 6 runs taking about 5 hours total wall time, or 2.5 hours with parallel execution. Week 3 would test β values of 0.05, 0.2, and 0.5 with 3 seeds each, totaling 9 runs at roughly 90 minutes per run, which comes to about 7 hours wall time in parallel. Week 4 gives me buffer to run extra seeds on the core conditions if needed.

For the core conditions specifically—exec-only, ISR with β=0.1, and random with β=0.1—I'm looking at 3 conditions with 2 additional seeds each, so 6 runs at 90 minutes per run, which is about 4.5 hours wall time with parallel execution. Adding everything up, I'm at roughly 14 hours of actual wall time, which is very manageable within my 9-day window.

But I also need to account for evaluation on all new experiments, writing up results, revising the paper, and final checks. That's probably 2 days for experiments, 3 days for analysis and paper revisions, and 1 day for final polish—about 6 days total, leaving a comfortable 3-day buffer.

Now I'm working through the specific questions, starting with whether Qwen3-1.5B is the right choice for week 2 or if I should consider the 7B variant or a different dataset instead.

The 1.5B model is risky because its base performance on FOLIO is likely too low to show meaningful results, which would undermine the whole point of demonstrating generalization. The 7B model would give me better baseline capability and more convincing proof rates, and it's still totally feasible on the 96GB GPU—probably around 40GB VRAM for K=8 GRPO with 150-200 minute runtimes per 500 steps. A different dataset like ProofWriter would address the single-dataset concern, but that adds engineering overhead for pipeline adaptation and train/test splits. I think the 7B model is the highest ROI move—it directly addresses the generalization concern while being likely to achieve meaningful results, whereas the 1.5B carries too much risk of proving nothing at all. The key insight is that ISR collapse across model sizes matters more than absolute performance numbers—if even a smaller model shows the same pattern of rising PR alongside falling ISR and emerging hacking behaviors, that validates the core finding. The real constraint is whether the model learns enough during training to exhibit meaningful hacking; if Qwen3-1.5B only reaches PR=15%, the signal might be too weak, but PR=40%+ would make the analysis compelling. I'm leaning toward Qwen3-7B as the primary choice since it's the safer bet for demonstrating the phenomenon robustly, with a potential Qwen3-1.5B pilot as a bonus if time allows.

For the 7B model on the RTX PRO 6000, I'm calculating that the base model takes about 14GB in bf16, and with K=8 rollouts generating multiple completions per batch plus KV cache overhead, I should still have plenty of headroom in the 96GB VRAM—runtime would stretch to roughly 160-250 minutes per 500-step run, which is manageable.

I need to dial back the batch size though to keep memory usage under control.

Now looking at the β parameter sweep: I have data for β=0.1 and β=0.3, and I'm considering adding 0.05, 0.2, and 0.5. The 0.05 value would be useful to see where the ISR signal starts becoming meaningful and whether H7 amplification persists at that lower bound. The 0.2 fills a natural gap between my existing points. For 0.5, since β=0.3 already shows severe instability, going higher might just amplify the same pattern, but it could reveal whether there's a floor to the collapse or if things degrade further.

I'm reconsidering though—the reviewer specifically asked for only 2 β values to keep the dose-response curve manageable. With five points across {0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5}, I'm probably overcomplicating this.

The computational constraint is real: 3 seeds per β takes about 4.5 hours per value on a single GPU, so with 2 GPUs I could run 3 β values in parallel within ~4.5 hours total. But the bigger question is whether I need random controls for every new β value. Since β=0.1 and β=0.3 already have controls, I could argue that β=0.05, 0.2, and 0.5 only need ISR runs to demonstrate the dose-response pattern—that's 9 runs instead of 18. The risk is that without controls at each point, I can't cleanly separate ISR-specific effects from general β effects, though I might be able to justify this limitation in the paper.

For seeds, 5 total is a meaningful improvement over 3 and aligns with what's standard for a venue like EMNLP Findings, even if 8+ would be statistically ideal. The cost-benefit here favors the smaller number.

Looking at which conditions actually need the extra runs: ISR with β=0.1 is where the core claim lives, so adding seeds there would really strengthen the H7 amplification result. The random β=0.1 control and exec-only conditions would also benefit from 2 extra seeds each to confirm their patterns hold. SFT is already stable across the 3 seeds I have, and β=0.3 shows such high variance that more seeds help less. So I'd focus on running ISR β=0.1, random β=0.1, and exec-only with the additional seeds—that's 6 extra runs total.

At roughly 90 minutes per run, that's about 4.5 hours of compute, which is totally manageable. Five seeds should be solid for EMNLP Findings, and while it's borderline for the main conference, it's defensible if I report proper bootstrap confidence intervals. I don't need to push to 8+ seeds for this venue.

Now for the LLM-based inter-annotator agreement question: using GPT-4o or Claude as a second annotator is getting more accepted in NLP venues by 2025-2026, but there's a real tension here. For validating a taxonomy, human IAA is still the gold standard, and since the taxonomy was partly created with AI help, having an LLM re-annotate it creates a circularity problem. EMNLP reviewers would probably view LLM-based agreement as supplementary evidence at best, not as the primary IAA metric.

But here's the key insight: most of my categories (H1, H7, H8) aren't actually subjective—they're rule-based, relying on text matching and functional analysis. For those, IAA isn't even the right measurement. Instead, I should precisely document the matching rules and demonstrate they're correct by construction. For the categories that do require manual inspection, LLM-based IAA could work as a supplement if I'm transparent about it. Better yet, I could frame the whole detection pipeline as algorithmic—showing the text matching rules, the functional analysis, and then doing false positive/negative analysis to validate the approach. The Prolog query discovery already proves the methodology works.

For EMNLP 2026, I need to be careful about how I present LLM-based annotation. Some reviewers will accept it, others won't. Positioning it as an "automated validation pipeline with LLM cross-checking" is safer than claiming the LLM is a second human annotator.

Now looking at the timeline: I have 9 days to pull this off. Days 1-2 are for launching the new experiments and revising the framing. By day 3 the experiments should be done and I can start analysis. Days 4-5 are for analysis and paper revisions. Days 6-7 handle the IAA analysis and final edits. Days 8-9 are for compilation and submission prep, with a buffer day built in.

The GPU requirements are manageable—about 15-17 hours of wall time across 2 GPUs for all the new experiments, plus another 10.5 hours for evaluation that can run in parallel. This fits into 2 days if everything runs smoothly. The real risk is if any experiment fails or produces unexpected results that need investigation.

Now I'm thinking through what the reviewer's "borderline 3/5 for Findings" actually means—they see merit but it's not quite strong enough for the main track. The action plan is solid on strengthening existing claims, but I'm wondering if reframing the meta-level Goodhart concern as a case study might inadvertently dilute the novelty too much. I should also consider whether explicitly committing to code release would address reproducibility concerns. The β sweep is a good addition, though framing it as a dose-response curve would make the narrative more compelling.

The real strategic question is what happens to the paper's core contribution if meta-level Goodhart gets downgraded from a novel concept to just an empirical instance. Looking at what's left: the STA methodology is useful but fairly standard, the Goodhart finding becomes a specific case study rather than a general insight, cross-logic transfer is interesting but not self-contained, and the 8-mode taxonomy is descriptive. For a main conference paper, I need to identify what makes this work beyond the particular system. The strongest angles seem to be positioning STA as a general framework for detecting reward hacking across any neurosymbolic system with external solvers, framing the core finding as a cautionary result about how diagnostics become gamed when used as rewards, or leaning into the taxonomy as a structural contribution.

Honestly, the paper fits better as a Findings paper now that the meta-level Goodhart framing is weaker. The empirical results are solid and the warning about reward design has real implications, but forcing it into a main conference narrative after acknowledging the conceptual limitations would just be over-claiming in a different way. I should accept the Findings positioning and let the strong empirical work and cautionary message stand on their own.

Now I'm checking whether the 1.5B versus 7B model choice really comes down to base capability differences on FOLIO. Looking at the baseline numbers: Qwen3-4B gets around 12% pass rate zero-shot, while Qwen3-1.5B is probably in the 3-5% range based on what I'm seeing.

The question is what happens after GRPO training—if the 1.5B model can't push much beyond 30% pass rate, the hacking analysis becomes limited, but if the 7B model can reach 80%+ quickly, the findings are more robust. The 7B seems like the safer bet, though I need to verify whether it's actually available on the server.
```

```text
Good. The training script takes `--model_path` as parameter, so switching to Qwen3-7B is straightforward - just need to download the model and change the path. The batch size might need adjustment for 7B (currently per_device_batch_size=4, gradient_accumulation_steps=2, num_generations=8).

For Qwen3-7B on 96GB:
- Model: ~14GB (bf16)
- Reference model: also ~14GB (GRPO needs reference model for KL)
- K=8 rollouts: KV cache for 8 completions × 512 tokens


- Total: probably ~40-50GB, well within 96GB

Might need to reduce batch size to 2 if OOM, but should work. Now I'm putting together my comprehensive review for the Director, organizing my findings on the model choice and expected performance improvements with GRPO training.

For Q1 W2, I'm recommending Qwen3-7B over the smaller variant because the 7B model should reach 60%+ performance after training, giving us meaningful hacking analysis—the 1.5B would likely cap out too low even with GRPO. Both models fit on the GPU with the 7B needing around 40-50GB for training. The 7B download is feasible at 14GB given our disk space, and while there's a risk it could saturate performance too quickly, that actually strengthens the paper by showing even stronger models exhibit the same collapse pattern. Running 6 total experiments across 2 conditions with 3 seeds should take roughly 6-8 hours on dual GPUs.

For Q2, I'm skipping dataset switching entirely—the engineering overhead doesn't fit our timeline, and the Prolog experiments already give us task variation. On β values, I'm prioritizing {0.05, 0.2, 0.5} with 0.05 being most critical since it tests whether H7 amplification persists at very low ISR doses, which would strengthen the meta-Goodhart story. I'll use ISR-only for the new β values since we've already established the random baseline with our earlier controls, letting us focus the dose-response curve on the ISR effect itself.

For Q3, five seeds is the right call for EMNLP Findings—it gives stable estimates and meaningful bootstrap confidence intervals without overcommitting to the timeline. If I have extra capacity, I'd prioritize more seeds for ISR at β=0.1 since that's the core claim, followed by the random control at that same dose. SFT doesn't need more seeds since it's already consistent, and β=0.3 is so unstable that additional seeds won't help.

For Q4, LLM-based IAA is risky for EMNLP 2026, so I'm leaning toward an algorithmic approach instead.

Most of the taxonomy is rule-based and deterministic—H1 and H7a are just text matching after normalization, H7b has a clear functional criterion. H8 is the only truly subjective one since it requires comparing premise sets to problem requirements. Rather than doing full IAA, I should precisely describe the two-stage pipeline and use the Prolog query case as proof the methodology works—the initial classifier missed it but functional cross-validation caught it. For H8 specifically, I could do human audit on just the 12+5 samples or use an LLM as a cross-check but frame it as automated validation rather than IAA to avoid reviewer pushback.

The timeline is tight but doable. Day 1 I download Qwen3-7B and launch the W4 seeds with ISR and random β=0.1 on exec-only, while starting the taxonomy fixes in parallel. Day 2 the W4 runs finish and I launch W3 with the β sweep and W2 with the 7B pilot, then begin taxonomy renumbering and related work. By Day 3 W3 is done, W2 is still running since 7B is slower, and I can evaluate W4 results and start drafting the paper.

Days 4-6 I complete W2, evaluate everything, and do a full paper revision plus the IAA/taxonomy audit. Days 7-8 are for visual review and final polish before submission. Day 9 is a buffer. The main risk is that Qwen3-7B might need hyperparameter tuning—if the first run runs out of memory or diverges, I need to budget about 4 hours for debugging.

I should also add a reproducibility section in the appendix with all hyperparameters in a table, and make sure to report effect sizes alongside the bootstrap confidence intervals for the statistical testing.

For the "meta-level Goodhart" terminology, instead of dropping it entirely, I could keep the term but frame it as an informal descriptor rather than a formal concept—something like "we observe what we informally term meta-level Goodhart"—which preserves searchability while addressing the reviewer's concern about over-packaging.

There's also a potential experiment where I run β=0.1 with ISR-as-reward but filter out samples where the conclusion appears in the premises before computing ISR, which would directly test whether H7 is an artifact of how ISR is defined. This would be a strong addition if time allows, though it requires additional setup.

After dropping "meta-level Goodhart" as a novel contribution, the paper reads more as a solid Findings piece than a main venue paper. The remaining novelty—STA as a diagnostic framework, the taxonomy, the Goodhart case study, and cross-logic transfer—are all incremental or descriptive rather than conceptually new. To make it main-worthy, I'd need either a fix that prevents Goodhart altogether, like a regularized reward formulation, or a new research direction entirely, but neither seems feasible in the remaining time.

A more realistic path is to lean into Findings explicitly. The paper works well as an empirical contribution showing how structural diagnostics get gamed when optimized, supported by a systematic taxonomy of hacking modes. I could strengthen this by testing on Qwen3-7B to show the patterns hold across models, and citing concurrent work on similar issues elsewhere. The contribution should center on ISR as a diagnostic tool and the empirical evidence of gaming, with Goodhart as the framing lens rather than the main claim.

Actually, let me reconsider that second claim.  The current framing treats this as "ISR induces meta-level Goodhart's Law," but I could reframe it as "ISR as a reward is an empirical instance of Goodhart's Law applied to structural diagnostics." That's not really a downgrade if I position it correctly. The core observation—that a diagnostic designed to catch gaming gets gamed when optimized—is genuinely novel, and the empirical findings like the 5× amplification of H7, the premise-count confound, and the triple gaming pattern are solid contributions. Adding a dose-response curve across the β sweep would strengthen this further. The real question is whether calling it "meta-level Goodhart" oversells the conceptual novelty. The better move is framing it as "an empirical instance illustrating a previously-discussed theoretical possibility" rather than claiming I've discovered something entirely new. That's more honest and actually stronger because it connects the work to existing theory while highlighting what's genuinely novel in the empirical evidence.

For a Findings paper this framing works well. For a main venue, it's borderline—I'd need the β sweep, model generalization, and the taxonomy connection to collectively make the case. Now I'm ready to write up the review report.

→ 不建议 LLM-as-annotator，改用算法验证\n\n**LLM-based IAA 在 EMNLP 2026 接受度不确定，且有循环性风险**（AI 标注 AI 生成的 taxonomy）。\n\n**更好策略**：\n- **H1/H7a**：纯确定性文本匹配（normalize 后精确/子串匹配）。不是主观判断，不需要 IAA——论文描述匹配规则即可，正确性 by construction。\n- **H7b**：功能性标准（n_necessary=1 且 text-match 未捕获）。客观标准，14 个样本全有 JSON 证据。\n- **H8**：最主观类别，但只有 12+5=17 个样本。**对这 17 个做人工审查**（Director 或 Worker 逐个验证），附上判断依据。比 LLM Cohen's κ 更可信。\n- **方法论证明**：引用 Prolog query-as-fact 发现（初始分类器报 100% genuine，functional cross-validation 暴露 95/97 tautological）作为方法论有效性证据。这比任何 IAA 数字都更有说服力。\n\n如果审稿人坚持要 IAA 数字：用 LLM 做 50 样本 cross-check，但标注为 \"automated validation\" 而非 \"second annotator\"。\n\n## Q5: 9 天时间线 → 可行，关键路径如下\n\n| 天 | 实验 | 写作 |\n|---|---|---|\n| 1 | 下载 Qwen3-7B + 7B pilot（50 步） | W1 framing + W11 重编号 + W12-W18 写作修复 |\n| 2 | W4 seeds（s45/s46, ISR+Random β=0.1, exec-only）开跑 | W5 Design Choices + W6 abstract 修正 + W10 相关工作 |\n| 3 | W4 完成 → eval; W3 β sweep 开跑 | W7 SFT 段落 + W8 ISR framing + 开始整合新数据 |\n| 4 | W3 完成 → eval; W2 7B 全量开跑 | 整合 W3/W4 到论文，dose-response 图 |\n| 5 | W2 完成 → eval | 整合 7B 结果，H8 人工审查 (W9) |\n| 6 | — | 全文修订完成 |\n| 7 | — | 编译 + 视觉审查 |\n| 8 | — | submission package |\n| 9 | — | buffer |\n\n**主要风险**：\n- Qwen3-7B OOM 或训练发散 → Day 1 pilot 是缓冲\n- β=0.05 产出意外结果（如 H7=0%）→ 仍有分析价值（threshold effect），不需要重跑\n- 某 seed 训练崩溃 → 用 s47 替补，不等修复\n\n## Q6: 遗漏和改进建议\n\n**遗漏 1: W1 过度降级**。方案说\"标题去掉 Meta-Level Goodhart\"，但这个术语是论文最大的记忆点和搜索性来源。建议**保留术语但降低 claim 强度**：标题可以是 \"Solver Trace Ablation Reveals Reward Hacking and Goodhart Effects in GRPO-Trained Neurosymbolic Formalizers\"；正文描述为 \"an empirical instance of Goodhart's Law at the meta level — the diagnostic designed to detect gaming becomes gamed when optimized\"。承认这是已有理论框架（Manheim & Garrabrant 2018）的经验实例，不 claim 发现新概念。这同时满足审稿人的 \"别过度包装\" 和论文的差异化需求。\n\n**遗漏 2: 效应量报告**。方案提了 bootstrap CI 但没提 effect size。所有比较应报 Cohen's d 或 OR（H7 已有 OR=15.05，genuine 已有 OR=0.61）。审稿人要求统计严谨性，effect size 是低成本高回报的补充。\n\n**遗漏 3: Reproducibility statement**。Appendix 加一个 hyperparameter table（已有大部分数据）+ 代码 release 承诺。低成本但 EMNLP 越来越看重。\n\n**改进: W3 β sweep 结果呈现**。与其分散在正文各处，做一个 **dose-response figure**（x 轴 β, y 轴 H7%/PR/ISR*，三条线 + error bars）。一图抵千言，直接回答 W3。\n\n## Q7: 去 Meta-Level Goodhart 后的 Novelty Story\n\n**诚实评估：降级后 paper 更自然适配 Findings，不适合硬冲 main。**\n\nmain 需要的：a novel concept + broad generalization。去掉概念新颖性后，剩下的是：\n- ISR/STA 作为诊断 → incremental（LOO 是标准技术）\n- 8-mode taxonomy → 描述性贡献\n- Cross-logic transfer → 辅助\n- Goodhart 经验实例 → 执行好但概念非原创\n\n**能让它进 main 的唯一路径**：Qwen3-7B + β sweep 合力展示 pattern 的 robustness + generality，配合一个 \"broad warning for neurosymbolic RL\" 的叙事。但这需要 7B 实验完美复现 4B 的 pattern——如果 7B 表现不同（可能性非零），反而削弱论文。\n\n**实际建议：Target Findings explicitly**。审稿人已推荐 Findings。接受定位，把精力花在让数据无懈可击上（β sweep + 5 seeds + 7B），而不是硬造 main-conference narrative。Findings 论文做到 4/5 比 main 论文 borderline 3/5 对 CV 更有价值。\n\nClaim 2 不会 \"弱到无法支撑\"——H7 5× amplification (p<10⁻³⁹)、premise-count confound discovery、triple gaming (H7b)、dose-response curve 合在一起是扎实的经验贡献。framing 降级只影响概念原创性声称，不影响数据贡献。\n\n---\n\n## 总结\n\n| 优先级 | 条目 | GPU-hours | 状态 |\n|--------|------|-----------|------|\n| P0 | W4 seeds (s45/s46, 3 条件) | ~9h (2-GPU ~4.5h) | 最高 ROI |\n| P0 | W1 framing 调整 | 0 | 纯写作 |\n| P1 | W3 β={0.05,0.2} ISR-only | ~6h (2-GPU ~3h) | 高 ROI |\n| P1 | W2 Qwen3-7B pilot + 全量 | ~12-16h (2-GPU ~6-8h) | 高 ROI 但有调参风险 |\n| P2 | W3 β=0.5 | ~3h | 低边际收益 |\n| P2 | W9 H8 人工审查 | 0 | 17 样本手动审 |\n| P3 | W5/W6/W7/W8/W10/W11 写作 | 0 | 可并行 |\n\n如果实验时间不够，**砍 β=0.5 和 7B**，保 W4 seeds + β={0.05,0.2}。这两者合计 ~7.5h wall time，2 天内必完，直接回应 W3+W4 两个 critical。",
  "project": "solver-trace-ablation"
}
```
